**Las PRV se alimentan desde los reservorios, según las conexiones del modelo nominal:**

- **R1 → PRV-1 → red principal.**
- **R2 → PRV-2 → red principal.**
- **Red principal → PRV-3 → sector regulado.**

El **tanque T1** se llena mediante **PUMP_1**, desde el nodo n54 de la red principal, y abastece otro sector por la tubería p239.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    R1[Reservorio R1] --> V1[PRV-1]
    R2[Reservorio R2] --> V2[PRV-2]
    V1 --> A[Red principal]
    V2 --> A
    A --> V3[PRV-3]
    V3 --> C[Sector regulado]
    A --> P[Bomba PUMP_1]
    P --> T[Tanque T1]
    T --> B[Sector abastecido por el tanque]
```

Por tanto, **T1 no es la fuente directa aguas arriba de las tres PRV**. Este esquema representa sus conexiones de diseño.
````

The Network Model — epanet-manual 2.2 Beta documentation (https://epanet-manual.readthedocs.io/en/latest/3_network_model.html)\nciteturn45search0 [wordlim: 200] Crawled: 5.7 years ago; EPANET computes in which of three different states a PRV can be in: ... >>   * partially opened (i.e., active) to achieve its pressure setting on its downstream side when the upstream pressure is above the setting>>   * fully open if the upstream pressure is below the setting\nValves\n\n> Valves are links that limit the pressure or flow at a specific point in the network. Their principal input parameters include:\n>\n>>   * start and end nodes\n>>   * diameter\n>>   * setting\n>>   * status.\n> \n> The computed outputs for a valve are flow rate and headloss. The different types of valves included in EPANET are:\n>\n>>   * Pressure Reducing Valve (PRV)\n>>   * Pressure Sustaining Valve (PSV)\n>>   * Pressure Breaker Valve (PBV)\n>>   * Flow Control Valve (FCV)\n>>   * Throttle Control Valve (TCV)\n>>   * General Purpose Valve (GPV).\n> \n> PRVs limit the pressure at a point in the pipe network. EPANET computes in which of three different states a PRV can be in:\n>\n>>   * partially opened (i.e., active) to achieve its pressure setting on its downstream side when the upstream pressure is above the setting\n>>   * fully open if the upstream pressure is below the setting\n>>   * closed if the pressure on the downstream side exceeds that on the upstream side (i.e., reverse flow is not allowed).\n> \n> PSVs maintain a set pressure at a specific point in the pipe network. EPANET computes in which of three different states a PSV can be in:\n>\n>>   * partially opened (i.e., active) to maintain its pressure setting on its upstream side when the downstream pressure is below this value\n>>   * fully open if the downstream pressure is above the setting\n>>   * closed if the pressure on the downstream side exceeds that on the upstream side (i.e., reverse flow is not allowed).\n> \n> PBVs force a specified pressure loss to occur across the valve. Flow through the valve can be in either direction. PBV’s are not true physical devices but can be used to model situations where a particular pressure drop is known to exist.\n> \n> FCVs limit the flow to a specified amount. The program produces a warning message if this flow cannot be maintained without having to add additional head at the valve (i.e., the flow cannot be maintained even with the valve fully open).\n> \n> TCVs simulate a partially closed valve by adjusting the minor head loss coefficient of the valve. A relationship between the degree to which a valve is closed and the resulting head loss coefficient is usually available from the valve manufacturer.\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\n6. Working with Objects — epanet-manual 2.2 Beta documentation (https://epanet-manual.readthedocs.io/en/latest/6_objects.html)\nciteturn45search1 [wordlim: 200] Crawled: 5.7 years ago; > Start Node  | The ID of the node on the nominal upstream or inflow side of the valve. ... >   * PRV - Pressure (psi or m) ... If a valve’s status was fixed to OPEN/CLOSED, then it can be made active again using a control that assigns a new numerical setting to it.\nThis is a required property.\n> End Node  | The ID of the node on the nominal downstream or discharge side of the valve. This is a required property.\n> Description  | An optional text string that describes other significant information about the valve.\n> Tag  | An optional text string (with no spaces) used to assign the valve to a category, perhaps based on type or location.\n> Diameter  | The valve diameter in inches (mm). This is a required property.\n> Type  | The valve type (PRV, PSV, PBV, FCV, TCV, or GPV). See Valves in Section 6.1 for descriptions of the various types of valves. This is a required property.\n> Setting  | A required parameter for each valve type that describes its operational setting:\n> \n>   * PRV - Pressure (psi or m)\n>   * PSV - Pressure (psi or m)\n>   * PBV - Pressure (psi or m)\n>   * FCV - Flow (flow units)\n>   * TCV - Loss Coeff (unitless)\n>   * GPV - ID of head loss curve\n> Loss Coefficient  | Unitless minor loss coefficient that applies when the valve is completely opened. Assumed 0 if left blank.\n> Fixed Status  | Valve status at the start of the simulation. If set to OPEN or CLOSED then the control setting of the valve is ignored and the valve behaves as an open or closed link, respectively. If set to NONE, then the valve will behave as intended. A valve’s fixed status and its setting can be made to vary throughout a simulation by the use of control statements. If a valve’s status was fixed to OPEN/CLOSED, then it can be made active again using a control that assigns a new numerical setting to it.\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\n13. ### Message

```text
**No podemos confirmar que n226 mantenga presión fija durante todo el dataset**, porque no tiene una serie de presión registrada en SCADA.

Lo que conocemos es la **consigna nominal de PRV-3: 35 m**. En el modelo ideal de EPANET:

- Si la PRV está **activa y dispone de suficiente presión aguas arriba**, regula n226 a esa consigna.
- Si no puede mantenerla, puede quedar completamente abierta y la presión dependerá de las condiciones hidráulicas. [Manual de EPANET](https://epanet-manual.readthedocs.io/en/latest/3_network_model.html).

Por tanto, **consigna constante no garantiza presión constante en todas las condiciones**. Para comprobar el comportamiento de n226 habría que analizar una simulación; sería presión simulada, distinta del objetivo de pronosticar presión registrada en SCADA.
```

The Network Modelcite1†¶ L9: \nL10: This chapter discusses how EPANET models the physical objects that constitute a distribution system as well as its operational parameters. Details about how this information is entered into the program are presented in later chapters. An overview is also given on the computational methods that EPANET uses to simulate hydraulic and water quality transport behavior.\nL11: \nL12: * * *\nL13: ## 3.1. Physical Componentscite2†¶ L14: \nL15: > EPANET models a water distribution system as a collection of links connected to nodes. The links represent pipes, pumps, and control valves. The nodes represent junctions, tanks, and reservoirs. The figure below illustrates how these objects can be connected to one another to form a network.\nL16: >\nL17: >> > cite3†Image: image30 L18: >>\nL19: >> Figure 3.1 Physical Components in a Water Distribution System\nL20: \nL21: Junctions\nL22: > Junctions are points in the network where links join together and where water enters or leaves the network. The basic input data required for junctions are:\nL23: >\nL24: >>   * elevation above some reference (usually mean sea level)\nL25: >>   * water demand (rate of withdrawal from the network)\nL26: >>   * initial water quality.\nL27: >\nL28: > The output results computed for junctions at all time periods of a simulation are:\nL29: >\nL30: >>   * hydraulic head (internal energy per unit weight of fluid)\nL31: >>   * pressure\nL32: >>   * water quality.\nL33: >\nL34: > Junctions can also:\nL35: >\nL36: >>   * have their demand vary with time\nL37: >>   * have multiple categories of demands assigned to them\nL38: >>   * have negative demands indicating that water is entering the network\nL39: >>   * have pressure dependent demand\nL40: >>   * be water quality sources where constituents enter the network\nL41: >>   * contain emitters (or sprinklers) which make the outflow rate depend on the pressure.\nL42: Reservoirs\nL43: > Reservoirs are nodes that represent an infinite external source or sink of water to the network. They are used to model such things as lakes, rivers, groundwater aquifers, and tie-ins to other systems. Reservoirs can also serve as water quality source points.\nL44: >\nL45: > The primary input properties for a reservoir are its hydraulic head (equal to the water surface elevation if the reservoir is not under pressure) and its initial quality for water quality analysis.\nL46: >\nL47: > Because a reservoir is a boundary point to a network, its head and water quality cannot be affected by what happens within the network. Therefore it has no computed output properties. However its head can be made to vary with time by assigning a time pattern to it (see Time Patterns below).\nL48: Tanks\nL49: > Tanks are nodes with storage capacity, where the volume of stored water can vary with time during a simulation. The primary input properties for tanks are:\nL50: >\nL51: >>   * bottom elevation (where water level is zero)\nL52: >>   * diameter (or shape if non-cylindrical )\nL53: >>   * initial, minimum and maximum water levels\nL54: >>   * initial water quality.\nL55: >\nL56: > The principal outputs computed over time are:\nL57: >\nL58: >>   * hydraulic head (water surface elevation)\nL59: >>   * water quality.\nL60: >\nL61: > Tanks are required to operate within their minimum and maximum levels. EPANET stops outflow if a tank is at its minimum level and stops inflow if it is at its maximum level. Tanks can also serve as water quality source points.\nL62: Emitters\nL63: > Emitters are devices associated with junctions that model the flow through a nozzle or orifice that discharges to the atmosphere. The flow rate through the emitter varies as a function of the pressure available at the node:\nL64: >\nL65: > \\[q = C p^{\\gamma}\\]\nL66: >\nL67: > where \\(q\\) = flow rate, \\(p\\) = pressure, \\(C\\) = discharge coefficient, and \\(\\gamma\\) = pressure exponent. For nozzles and sprinkler heads \\(\\gamma\\) equals 0.5 and the manufacturer usually provides the value of the discharge coefficient in units of gpm/psi ^{0.5} (stated as the flow through the device at a 1 psi pressure drop).\nL68: >\nL69: > Emitters are used to model flow through sprinkler systems and irrigation networks. They can also be used to simulate leakage in a pipe connected to the junction (if a discharge coefficient and pressure exponent for the leaking crack or joint can be estimated) or compute a fire flow at the junction (the flow available at some minimum residual pressure).\nL70: In the latter case one would use a very high value of the discharge coefficient (e.g., 100 times the maximum flow expected) and modify the junction’s elevation to include the equivalent head of the pressure target. EPANET treats emitters as a property of a junction and not as a separate network component.\nL71: >\nL72: > Note\nL73: >\nL74: > The pressure-flow relation at a junction defined by an emitter should not be confused with the pressure-demand relation when performing a pressure dependent analysis (PDA). See Section: cite4†Pressure Dependent Demand Model for more information.\nL75: Pipes\nL76: > Pipes are links that convey water from one point in the network to another. EPANET assumes that all pipes are full at all times. Flow direction is from the end at higher hydraulic head (internal energy per weight of water) to that at lower head. The principal hydraulic input parameters for pipes are:\nL77: >\nL78: >>   * start and end nodes\nL79: >>   * diameter\nL80: >>   * length\nL81: >>   * roughness coefficient (for determining headloss)\nL82: >>   * status (open, closed, or contains a check valve).\nL83: >\nL84: > The status parameter allows pipes to implicitly contain shutoff (gate) valves and check (non-return) valves (which allow flow in only one direction).\nL85: >\nL86: > The water quality inputs for pipes consist of:\nL87: >\nL88: >>   * bulk reaction coefficient\nL89: >>   * wall reaction coefficient.\nL90: >\nL91: > These coefficients are explained more thoroughly in Section 3.4 below.\nL92: >\nL93: > Computed outputs for pipes include:\nL94: >\nL95: >>   * flow rate\nL96: >>   * velocity\nL97: >>   * headloss\nL98: >>   * Darcy-Weisbach friction factor\nL99: >>   * average reaction rate (over the pipe length)\nL100: >>   * average water quality (over the pipe length).\nL101: >\nL102: > The hydraulic head lost by water flowing in a pipe due to friction with the pipe walls can be computed using one of three different formulas:\nL103: >\nL104: >>   * Hazen-Williams formula\nL105: >>   * Darcy-Weisbach formula\nL106: >>   * Chezy-Manning formula\nL107: >\nL108: > The Hazen-Williams formula is the most commonly used headloss formula in the US. It cannot be used for liquids other than water and was originally developed for turbulent flow only. The Darcy-Weisbach formula is the most theoretically correct. It applies over all flow regimes and to all liquids. The Chezy-Manning formula is more commonly used for open channel flow.\nL109: >\nL110: > Each formula uses the following equation to compute headloss between the start and end node of the pipe:\nL111: >\nL112: >> \\[h_{L} = A q^{B}\\]\nL113: >\nL114: > where \\(h_{L}\\) = headloss (Length), \\(q\\) = flow rate (Volume/Time), \\(A\\) = resistance coefficient, and \\(B\\) = flow exponent. Table 3.1 lists expressions for the resistance coefficient and values for the flow exponent for each of the formulas. Each formula uses a different pipe roughness coefficient that must be determined empirically. Table 3.2 lists general ranges of these coefficients for different types of new pipe materials.\nL115: Be aware that a pipe’s roughness coefficient can change considerably with age.\nL116: >\nL117: > With the Darcy-Weisbach formula EPANET uses different methods to compute the friction factor f depending on the flow regime:\nL118: >\nL119: >>   * The Hagen–Poiseuille formula is used for laminar flow (Re < 2,000).\nL120: >>   * The Swamee and Jain approximation to the Colebrook-White equation is used for fully turbulent flow (Re > 4,000).\nL121: >>   * A cubic interpolation from the Moody Diagram is used for transitional flow (2,000 < Re < 4,000) .\nL122: >\nL123: > Consult cite5†Chapter 13 for the actual equations used.\nL124: >\nL125: >> Table 3.1 Pipe Headloss Formulas for Full Flow (for headloss in feet and flow rate in cfs)\nL126: >>\nL127: >> Formula  | Resistance Coefficient (\\(A\\))  | Flow Exponent (\\(B\\))\nL128: >> --- | --- | ---\nL129: >> Hazen-Williams  | \\(4.727\\,C^{-1.852}\\,d^{-4.871}\\,L\\)  | \\(1.852\\)\nL130: >> Darcy-Weisbach  | \\(0.0252\\,f(\\epsilon,d,q)\\,d ^{-5}\\,L\\)  | \\(2\\)\nL131: >> Chezy-Manning  | \\(4.66\\,n^{2}\\,d^{-5.33}\\,L\\)  | \\(2\\)\nL132: >>\nL133: >> Notes:\nL134: >>\nL135: >>> \\(C\\) = Hazen-Williams roughness coefficient\nL136: >>>\nL137: >>> \\(\\epsilon\\) = Darcy-Weisbach roughness coefficient (ft)\nL138: >>>\nL139: >>> \\(f\\) = friction factor (dependent on \\(\\epsilon\\), \\(d\\), and \\(q\\))\nL140: >>>\nL141: >>> \\(n\\) = Manning roughness coefficient\nL142: >>>\nL143: >>> \\(d\\) = pipe diameter (ft)\nL144: >>>\nL145: >>> \\(L\\) = pipe length (ft)\nL146: >>>\nL147: >>> \\(q\\) = flow rate (cfs)\nL148: >>\nL149: >> Table 3.2 Roughness Coefficients for New Pipe\nL150: >>\nL151: >> Material  | Hazen-Williams \\(C\\) (unitless)  | Darcy-Weisbach \\(\\epsilon\\) (ft x \\(10^{-3}\\))  | Manning’s \\(n\\) (unitless)\nL152: >> --- | --- | --- | ---\nL153: >> Cast Iron  | 130 – 140  | 0.85  | 0.012 – 0.015\nL154: >> Concrete or Concrete Lined  | 120 – 140  | 1.0 – 10  | 0.012 – 0.017\nL155: >> Galvanized Iron  | 120  | 0.5  | 0.015 – 0.017\nL156: >> Plastic  | 140 – 150  | 0.005  | 0.011 – 0.015\nL157: >> Steel  | 140 – 150  | 0.15  | 0.015 – 0.017\nL158: >> Vitrified Clay  | 110  |    | 0.013 – 0.015\nL159: >\nL160: > Pipes can be set open or closed at preset times or when specific conditions exist, such as when tank levels fall below or above certain set points, or when nodal pressures fall below or above certain values. See the discussion of Controls in Section 3.2.\nL161: Minor Losses\nL162: > Minor head losses (also called local losses) are caused by the added turbulence that occurs at bends and fittings. The importance of including such losses depends on the layout of the network and the degree of accuracy required. They can be accounted for by assigning the pipe a minor loss coefficient. The minor headloss becomes the product of this coefficient and the velocity head of the pipe, i.e.,\nL163: >\nL164: > \\[h_L = K (\\frac{v^2}{2g})\\]\nL165: >\nL166: > where \\(K\\) = minor loss coefficient, \\(v\\) = flow velocity (Length/Time), and \\(g\\) = acceleration of gravity (Length/Time ^{2}). Table 3.3 provides minor loss coefficients for several types of fittings.\nL167: >\nL168: >> Table 3.3 Minor Loss Coefficients for Selected Fittings\nL169: >>\nL170: >> FITTING  | LOSS COEFFICIENT\nL171: >> --- | ---\nL172: >> Globe valve, fully open  | 10.0\nL173: >> Angle valve, fully open  | 5.0\nL174: >> Swing check valve, fully open  | 2.5\nL175: >> Gate valve, fully open  | 0.2\nL176: >> Short-radius elbow  | 0.9\nL177: >> Medium-radius elbow  | 0.8\nL178: >> Long-radius elbow  | 0.6\nL179: >> 45 degree elbow  | 0.4\nL180: >> Closed return bend  | 2.2\nL181: >> Standard tee - flow through run  | 0.6\nL182: >> Standard tee - flow through branch  | 1.8\nL183: >> Square entrance  | 0.5\nL184: >> Exit  | 1.0\nL185: Pumps\nL186: > Pumps are links that impart energy to a fluid thereby raising its hydraulic head. The principal input parameters for a pump are its start and end nodes and its pump curve (the combination of heads and flows that the pump can produce). In lieu of a pump curve, the pump could be represented as a constant energy device, one that supplies a constant amount of energy (horsepower or kilowatts) to the fluid for all combinations of flow and head.\nL187: >\nL188: > The principal output parameters are flow and head gain. Flow through a pump is unidirectional and EPANET will not allow a pump to operate outside the range of its pump curve.\nL189: >\nL190: > Variable speed pumps can also be considered by specifying that their speed setting be changed under these same types of conditions. By definition, the original pump curve supplied to the program has a relative speed setting of 1. If the pump speed doubles, then the relative setting would be 2; if run at half speed, the relative setting is 0.5 and so on. Changing the pump speed shifts the position and shape of the pump curve (see the section on Pump Curves below).\nL191: >\nL192: > As with pipes, pumps can be turned on and off at preset times or when certain conditions exist in the network. A pump’s operation can also be described by assigning it a time pattern of relative speed settings. EPANET can also compute the\nL193: >\nL194: > energy consumption and cost of a pump. Each pump can be assigned an efficiency curve and schedule of energy prices. If these are not supplied then a set of global energy options will be used.\nL195: >\nL196: > Flow through a pump is unidirectional. If system conditions require more head than the pump can produce, EPANET shuts the pump off. If more than the maximum flow is required, EPANET extrapolates the pump curve to the required flow, even if this produces a negative head. In both cases a warning message will be issued.\nL197: Valves\nL198: > Valves are links that limit the pressure or flow at a specific point in the network. Their principal input parameters include:\nL199: >\nL200: >>   * start and end nodes\nL201: >>   * diameter\nL202: >>   * setting\nL203: >>   * status.\nL204: >\nL205: > The computed outputs for a valve are flow rate and headloss. The different types of valves included in EPANET are:\nL206: >\nL207: >>   * Pressure Reducing Valve (PRV)\nL208: >>   * Pressure Sustaining Valve (PSV)\nL209: >>   * Pressure Breaker Valve (PBV)\nL210: >>   * Flow Control Valve (FCV)\nL211: >>   * Throttle Control Valve (TCV)\nL212: >>   * General Purpose Valve (GPV).\nL213: >\nL214: > PRVs limit the pressure at a point in the pipe network. EPANET computes in which of three different states a PRV can be in:\nL215: >\nL216: >>   * partially opened (i.e., active) to achieve its pressure setting on its downstream side when the upstream pressure is above the setting\nL217: >>   * fully open if the upstream pressure is below the setting\nL218: >>   * closed if the pressure on the downstream side exceeds that on the upstream side (i.e., reverse flow is not allowed).\n"

| **Consigna nominal, cotas y tuberías** | Describen la relación física | Contexto hidráulico; son parámetros estáticos |

La utilidad de **n229 no está garantizada**: mientras PRV-3 regula correctamente, puede amortiguar las variaciones aguas arriba.

Hay una relación física interesante. **Si PRV-3 está activa a 35 m**, usando las cotas nominales:

\[
p_{215}=35+(6.1130-2.0066)-h_L
       =39.1064-h_L
\]

Aquí \(h_L\) es la pérdida de carga entre n226 y n215, en metros. Por eso n215 puede tener aproximadamente **39 m**, aunque la consigna sea 35 m. Esa pérdida depende del comportamiento hidráulico del sector. [Manual de EPANET](https://epanet-manual.readthedocs.io/en/latest/3_network_model.html).

**Nos faltan mediciones directas** del caudal por PRV-3, de la demanda del sector, de la presión n226 y del estado operativo de la válvula. Los caudales generales y el nivel T1 serían información indirecta.

Empezaría comparando **historia de n215 + calendario**, y después añadiría **historia de n229** para medir su aporte. La tarea exige una comparación cuidadosa: en enero–septiembre de 2018, n215 varía entre **38,89 y 39,10 m**, y la persistencia a +15 minutos tiene un MAE descriptivo de apenas **0,0051 m**. Estos son cálculos de exploración, no resultados finales de prueba.

[('training','2018-01-01','2018-09-30'),('validation','2018-10-01','2018-12-31')]:\n x=joined.loc[a:b]\n print('PERIOD',name,len(x))\n for node in ['n1','n4','n31']:\n  err=(x[node].shift(-3)-x[node]).dropna()\n  print(json.dumps({'node':node,'range_m':[float(x[node].min()),float(x[node].max())],'std_m':float(x[node].std()),'persist15_MAE_m':float(err.abs().mean()),'corr_level':float(x[node].corr(x.T1)),'corr_pump':float(x[node].corr(x.PUMP_1)),'pipe_distance_T1_m':dist[node],'elevation_m':elev[node]},ensure_ascii=False))\nprint('CORRELATIONS_TRAIN',joined.loc['2018-01-01':'2018-09-30',['n1','n4','n31','T1','PUMP_1']].corr().round(4).to_dict())\nprint('TANK',sections['[TANKS]'])\n'@ Tsinghua University\nL24: ## Description\nL25: Drinking Water Distribution Networks (DWDN) are susceptible to infrastructure failures, which may lead to water losses. Typically, these water losses are due to background leakages and pipe bursts which may occur anywhere within the distribution network. Background leakages are normally difficult to detect due to their small size, whereas pipe bursts are easier to locate as they are of larger size and may appear on the surface.\nL26: The early detection and localization of some leakage event is extremely important, as this would reduce the time required for accommodating the event and therefore reducing the risk of further infrastructure degradation, contamination events and consumer complaints.\nL27: In previous years, a number of methodologies have been proposed to detect and isolate the location of leakage events using various types of sensor measurements. These methods were commonly evaluated on private commercial datasets, and as a result, it is not possible to objectively compare these methods in their ability to detect and isolate leaks.\nL28: In the past year, a leakage detection dataset has been proposed, LeakDB, based on benchmark networks and created using the WNTR tool, using pressure-driven demands and realistic leakage modelling. Inspired by the “BATtle of the Attack Detection ALgorithms” (BATADAL), which focused on the detection of cyber-physical attacks, our team decided to organize a similar “battle” focusing on leakage events.\nL29: The Battle of the Leakage Detection and Isolation Methods (BattLeDIM),  aims at objectively comparing the performance of methods for the detection and localization of leakage events, relying on SCADA measurements of flow and pressure sensors installed within water distribution networks. Participants may use different types of tools and methods, including (but not limited to) engineering judgement, machine learning, statistical methods, signal processing, and model-based fault diagnosis approaches.\nL30: ## Files\nL31: \nL32: ###\nL33: \nL34: 2018_Fixed_Leakages_Report.txt\nL35: \nL36: cite11†Preview L37: ###\nL38: \nL39: Files (550.5 MB)\nL40: Name  | Size  |\nL41: --- ### Message

```text
**Centrarnos en el Área C tiene sentido. Tomaría n1 como candidato principal**, por su mayor distancia de T1 y su menor presión entre los tres sensores.

La distinción hidráulica es que **PUMP_1 llena T1; el tanque abastece el sector**. El nivel aporta información sobre la carga disponible y el caudal de la bomba sobre la dinámica de llenado. La salida del tanque hacia el sector no tiene caudal medido. Los archivos provienen de la [fuente oficial de BattLeDIM](https://zenodo.org/records/4017659).

Calculé esta comparación con **enero–septiembre de 2018**:

| Nodo | Distancia nominal por tuberías desde T1 | Rango de presión | Correlación con nivel T1 |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| **n1** | **891 m** | **25,67–29,36 m** | **0,938** |
| n4 | 574 m | 30,90–34,31 m | 0,941 |
| n31 | 308 m | 34,59–37,57 m | 0,979 |

Para pronosticar **n1 a +15 minutos**, disponemos de:

- Historia de presión de **n1**, más **n4 y n31**.
- Nivel **T1**, sus rezagos y cambios recientes.
- Caudal **PUMP_1** actual e histórico.
- Demandas AMR —incluidas n1/n4/n31— y calendario.

Compararía la historia de n1 sola con la incorporación gradual de tanque/bomba, presiones vecinas y AMR. Así podremos medir qué información aporta realmente. **Estas relaciones no demuestran todavía mejora predictiva ni novedad.**

Dejé la selección provisional y las [gráficas](<D:/GitHub/ML NJIT/MiniProject/Milestone 2/respaldo/estado_del_arte/seleccion_area_c/area_c_candidate_analysis.png>) documentadas en la [comparación detallada](<D:/GitHub/ML NJIT/MiniProject/Milestone 2/notas/13_AREA_C_SELECCION_TARGET.md>), sin utilizar 2019 para elegir el sensor.
